Scenario C (Azure disappoints): Stock goes to $375-380. You're 20% positioned, you stop out at $385 with 2.2% loss. You've protected yourself but given up all the upside from Scenarios A and B to avoid this tail risk.

The conservative sizing mathematically underperforms in both favorable outcomes to protect against a tail risk that's only 10-15% probable.

The neutral analyst's 40% position with 35% powder for tactical adds is closer, but he's still making the same mistake: he's treating a base-case thesis as though it's uncertain and positioning accordingly.

My Counterargument to the Position Sizing Objection¶

The conservative and neutral analysts both object to 75% sizing on grounds that it's "overcommitted ahead of earnings." But here's what they're missing:

If Scenario B (65% probable) is the base case and offers 8-12% upside, then 75% sizing is the correct sizing relative to the conviction level and expected return.

Here's the risk math:

  • Expected return: 65% × 10% = +6.5% (base case upside)
  • Tail risk: 35% × (-15% average across A and C) = -5.25%
  • Net expected return: +1.25% conservative estimate

But wait—that assumes you stop out perfectly at $354 with zero slippage. The reality is that if the thesis breaks, you'd probably stop at $375-385 (a 4.6% loss), not $354. Let me recalculate:

  • Expected return: 65% × 10% = +6.5%
  • Tail risk: 25% (Scenario A) × 0% (you add upside, not downside risk) = 0%
  • Tail risk: 10% (Scenario C) × (-4.6%) = -0.46%
  • Net expected return: +6.04%

A 75% position with 4.6% maximum downside and 6%+ expected return is exactly the right sizing.

The conservative and neutral analysts are treating the 4.6% downside as "unacceptable risk" when they should be treating it as "reasonable risk for 6%+ expected return and 65% probability."

The Real Problem With Conservative Risk Management in This Market¶

Here's what I think is actually happening: both the conservative and neutral analysts are optimizing for not looking foolish if the thesis breaks. They're sizing defensively to protect their reputations, not to maximize risk-adjusted returns.

This is the classic problem with risk management frameworks that reward managers for not losing money above all else: they systematically undersize winning positions to protect against tail risks, and over time, they underperform because they're always undercommitted when the thesis is right.

The trader's original proposal to buy 75% at $393 with a $354 stop and 25% powder for confirmation is not reckless. It's appropriately bold for a thesis where:

  • The risk is managed (10% stop loss, scaling plan)
  • The timeframe is appropriate (12-18 months)

The Conservative Analyst's Deferred Revenue Argument Doesn't Actually Undermine The Capex Case¶

Let me address the conservative analyst's core objection directly, because this is where the neutral analyst has sided with weakness.

Let me walk through the actual business cycle:

  1. Customer commits to multi-year Azure contract → This shows up as deferred revenue increase
  2. Microsoft deploys infrastructure to support that workload → This is capex, recognized immediately
  3. Over the next 1-3 years, Microsoft recognizes the revenue → This shows up as revenue and operating cash flow

Now, here's the critical point: if Microsoft just won the customer commitments last quarter (implied by deferred revenue growth), then the capex to support those workloads would be deployed this quarter and future quarters. That means we should expect capex to exceed deferred revenue growth as Microsoft front-loads infrastructure to serve committed workloads.

The 84% capex growth versus 15% deferred revenue growth is not a divergence. It's the expected pattern. It's Microsoft saying "we just won major customer commitments (deferred revenue), and now we're deploying infrastructure to support them (capex)."

The conservative analyst is looking at this data and saying "something's wrong." I'm looking at it and saying "something's right—the company is executing on its Azure growth thesis."

The neutral analyst accepts the conservative framing, which is where his recommendation loses credibility.

The Free Cash Flow Deterioration Is Temporary And Cyclical, Not Structural¶

Microsoft's fiscal year ends March 31. Q1 FY2026 (March 2026) is fiscal year-end. This is when annual true-ups, pension contributions, and tax payments happen. This is when working capital typically compresses. This is the seasonally weakest cash flow quarter for enterprise software companies.

The neutral analyst acknowledges "Q1 is seasonally weak" but then doesn't fully apply this insight. He should, because it undermines the conservative case.

  • TTM Capex: ~$94B annually (this is elevated due to the Q1 spike)
  • TTM FCF: ~$91B annually

Now, $91B in annual free cash flow is enormous. The company is generating $91B annually after investing $94B in capex. This is not a cash generation model breaking. This is a company deploying massive capital while still generating over $90B in free cash flow.

The conservative analyst keeps pointing to Q1's $15.80B FCF and saying "the model is broken." But that's one seasonal quarter. The TTM is what matters, and the TTM shows $91B in FCF generation.

Even if we assume elevated capex continues at $30B per quarter, that's $120B annually. Against $184B in OCF, that leaves $64B in FCF annually. Subtract $27B in dividends, and Microsoft still has $37B annually for buybacks, debt reduction, or additional investment. That's not broken. That's fortress-grade cash generation.

The neutral analyst has accepted the conservative framing on FCF when the actual numbers don't support it.

The difference between 55% and 70% is +0.74% of expected return, with only +0.08% additional downside risk. That's a favorable asymmetry. You're capturing 28% more upside (+0.74% vs +2.69% base) while taking only 25% more downside risk (+0.40% vs +0.32% base).

The Aggressive Analyst's "Deferred Revenue" Argument Is Circular Logic Dressed Up as Evidence¶

The aggressive analyst keeps pointing to deferred revenue hitting $50.92B, up from $44.2B year-over-year, and claiming this proves Azure demand justifies the capex. I need to expose exactly how misleading this is.

Yes, deferred revenue grew from $44.2B to $50.92B. That's a 15.2% increase. Now let me ask the critical question: did capex grow 15% or 84%? It grew 84%. That's not alignment. That's divergence.

Deferred revenue growth of 15% does not justify capex acceleration of 84%. Period. The aggressive analyst is using deferred revenue as a proxy for "customer demand," but he's confusing deferred revenue with commitment intensity. Customers can commit to more cloud services while growing their commitments at 15% because they're spreading spending across multiple quarters and years. That's not the same as justifying an 84% year-over-year jump in capex spending right now.

Here's what the aggressive analyst won't say: if deferred revenue is only growing 15%, then the capex increase is outpacing revenue recognition by a massive margin. That's exactly the pattern we saw with telecommunications companies in 2000—massive capex, moderate revenue growth, and margin compression as the ROI story deteriorated.

The circular logic is this: the aggressive analyst says "management wouldn't approve this capex unless they had visibility into demand," then uses deferred revenue growth as "evidence" of that demand, but deferred revenue is growing slower than capex. So the evidence actually cuts the other direction. The divergence between 84% capex growth and 15% deferred revenue growth is not bullish. It's a warning sign that management is overcommitting.

The "Seasonality" Argument for Free Cash Flow is Intellectually Convenient, Not Factually Grounded¶

The aggressive analyst wants to explain away the deterioration in free cash flow—from $25.66B in Q3 to $25.57B in Q2 to only $15.80B in Q1—by invoking "seasonal working capital flows at fiscal year-end."

Here's the problem: that's an excuse, not an explanation. Yes, Q1 is Microsoft's fiscal year-end. Yes, there are timing dynamics. But the data actually shows something more concerning than seasonality.

Let me walk through the actual numbers:

  • Q3 FY2025 (Sep 2025): OCF $45.06B, Capex $19.39B, FCF $25.66B, Capex/OCF = 43%
  • Q2 FY2025 (Jun 2025): OCF $42.65B, Capex $17.08B, FCF $25.57B, Capex/OCF = 40%
  • Q1 FY2026 (Mar 2026): OCF $46.68B, Capex $30.88B, FCF $15.80B, Capex/OCF = 66%

The aggressive analyst says this is seasonal. But look at what's actually happening: capex as a percentage of operating cash flow jumped from 40-43% to 66%. That's not seasonal variation. That's a structural shift.

Even if we grant that Q1 is seasonally weak for FCF (which is debatable), the trend is unmistakable: capex intensity has accelerated structurally. The ratio of capex to OCF has increased by 50% quarter-over-quarter. That's not noise. That's a change in how the company is deploying capital.

And here's the part that should concern us: if this capex intensity persists, and if OCF growth slows (which is entirely plausible if cloud growth decelerates), the company faces a cash generation squeeze. The aggressive analyst dismisses this by saying "even at elevated capex, you have $15-30B quarterly in FCF." But he's not accounting for what happens if OCF plateaus while capex stays elevated.

What if OCF stays at $43-46B and capex stays at $30B per quarter? Then FCF is $13-16B quarterly, or $52-64B annually. That's still enough for dividends (~$27B annual run rate) and modest buybacks, but it leaves no room for debt reduction, special returns, or unexpected capex increases. That's not a fortress. That's a company running at the edge of its cash generation capacity.

The Neutral Analyst's Probability Weightings Are Fundamentally Wrong¶

Now, the neutral analyst claims to be neutral, but his probability weightings reveal a bullish bias that he's not acknowledging. He assigns Scenario A (Azure 28%+) a 45% probability, then later revises it to 55-60% based on "analyst consensus."

Let me reweight the scenarios based on actual risk assessment, not consensus optimism:

Scenario A (Azure 28%+): 35-40% probability. Why? Because while analyst consensus is bullish, the actual hard data shows deferred revenue growing only 15%, which is respectable but not exceptional. Scenario C (Azure under 22%): 15-20% probability. This is the tail case, but it's not zero. If macro concerns surface (corporate IT budget pressures, competitive loss to AWS on specific workloads), the earnings call could be disappointing. In this case, the downside is sharp.

Here's What I'm Actually Concerned About: The Consensus Trap¶

Both the aggressive and neutral analysts are heavily weighted to "60+ analysts have buy ratings," which they cite as evidence of fundamental strength. I want to expose exactly how dangerous this reasoning is.

In 2007, homebuilders like Centex and Toll Brothers had consensus buy ratings as housing prices peaked. Analysts were bullish because earnings were strong and the economy looked fine. What they missed was the mortgage collapse coming.

In 2000, tech stocks like Cisco and Nortel had 40+ buy ratings as capex spending accelerated. Analysts were bullish because revenue growth was strong and internet adoption was accelerating. What they missed was that capex spending didn't translate to proportional revenue growth, and margins compressed for years.

In 2022, Meta had analyst consensus tilting bullish even as user growth stalled, because the company still had fortress cash generation. The stock fell 70% anyway as growth slowed and capex failed to drive returns.

My point is this: analyst consensus is a lagging indicator, not a leading indicator. By the time 60+ analysts agree on something, the market has already priced in a significant portion of the positive thesis. You're not getting information about what will happen. You're getting information about what the market expects to happen.

And in the case of MSFT, the market expectations are very high. The stock is at $395.71 after a 12.2% rally from lows. It's trading at 23.53x trailing P/E and 20.42x forward P/E. The PEG of 1.19 assumes 23% EPS growth continues. None of this is cheap. All of this is priced for near-perfection on Azure.

The Earnings Catalyst Timing Argument Is Actually Backward¶

The aggressive analyst says we need to be 75% positioned before earnings because "if earnings come in with 28%+ Azure growth, the stock will move immediately and we'll have missed it."

If Azure growth disappoints (under 25%), the stock will gap down 3-5% on earnings day. Being 75% positioned heading into this scenario is exactly the wrong posture. We'd be underwater immediately, forced to either cut the position (locking in losses) or hold and watch the unrealized loss grow.

The time-critical argument doesn't actually favor 75% pre-earnings positioning. It favors appropriate positioning for an uncertain catalyst. And appropriate positioning for an uncertain catalyst is 30%, not 75%.

The Deferred Revenue "Reframing" Is Sophistry, Not Analysis¶

Deferred revenue represents cash already collected from customers for future service delivery. It is the most leading indicator of future revenue recognition we have. For a 25% position (my recommendation): - Scenario A: 25% × 20% × +12% upside = +0.6% expected value - Scenario B: 25% × 55% × +3% upside = +0.41% expected value - Scenario C: 25% × 25% × -10% downside = -0.63% expected value - Net expected return: +0.38% with asymmetric tail risk

Wait, that's negative expected value. Let me reconsider.

Here's What the Aggressive Analyst Gets Right (And What He's Misusing)¶

The aggressive analyst is correct on the fundamentals. Microsoft's 38% net margins, 34% ROE, 18% revenue growth, and 23% EPS growth are real and exceptional. The analyst consensus of 60+ buy ratings is real. The company's fortress cash generation is real. These are not invented facts.

Here's the thing: strong fundamentals don't prove that an 84% year-over-year capex increase is justified. They prove that the company can afford the capex. Those are different statements. A fortress balance sheet can support wasteful capital deployment just as easily as it can support smart capital deployment. The balance sheet strength tells us Microsoft won't go bankrupt. It doesn't tell us Azure capex will generate acceptable ROI.

Here's What the Conservative Analyst Gets Right (And Where He Overextends)¶

The conservative analyst is correct that the divergence between 84% capex growth and 15% deferred revenue growth is a warning signal. That divergence is real, and it deserves serious consideration. The conservative analyst is also correct that analyst consensus is a lagging indicator, not a leading indicator—the 60+ buy ratings are reflecting what the market already expects, not revealing hidden information.

But the conservative analyst makes a logical leap that I need to challenge: he assumes that this divergence proves capex is unjustified. That's too strong a conclusion from the data. The divergence is concerning, but it doesn't prove anything yet. Here's why:

Deferred revenue can grow at a slower rate than capex for legitimate reasons. Microsoft might be investing heavily in infrastructure now to support revenue growth that will be recognized over time. Capex is a point-in-time expense. Deferred revenue is cumulative. The timing mismatch doesn't necessarily mean the investment isn't justified—it might just mean the payoff is delayed.

The conservative analyst cites Cisco, IBM, and telecommunications companies as historical precedents for capex disappointment. But here's where that argument breaks down: those companies faced competitive displacement (Cisco lost market share to newer players, telcos faced disruption). Microsoft isn't facing that threat in Azure. Amazon AWS is the incumbent, but Microsoft isn't fighting for second place—Microsoft is competing for specific workloads and customer segments where Azure has advantages (Office 365 integration, enterprise relationships, AI stack with OpenAI).

The precedents matter only if the competitive dynamics are similar. They're not.

Here's What Both Analysts Are Missing: The Actual Data on Azure Momentum¶

Both analysts are debating probability weightings without looking at the actual Azure growth trajectory. Let me be specific about what the data shows:

According to the earnings reports and analyst commentary, Azure organic growth has been in the 23-25% range for the past several quarters. This is strong growth for a cloud platform at scale. Now, the question is: will this growth accelerate to 28%+ (Scenario A), stay in-line at 22-26% (Scenario B), or decelerate to under 22% (Scenario C)?

Here's what the data actually tells us:

  • Analyst consensus is for 25-27% Azure growth in the quarter we're about to hear results for. Not 28%+. Not 30%+. 25-27%. That's the consensus expectation.

Microsoft's Other Cloud Services (like GitHub Copilot, Dynamics Cloud) are also included. When you separate them out, Azure-specific deferred revenue is probably running at 18-22%, which aligns with 23-25% organic growth.
  • The capex acceleration is occurring specifically to support AI workloads within Azure. This is not generic infrastructure. This is competitive necessity in a market where Google and Amazon are also deploying massive AI infrastructure. Both analysts have been treating the capex question as though it's binary: either capex is justified or it isn't. Here's what I'm going to do: I'm going to acknowledge where each analyst is genuinely right, identify where each is overextending beyond the evidence, and then recommend what I actually believe is the most defensible position given the ambiguity we genuinely face.

Where the Aggressive Analyst Is Actually Correct¶

Let me start by giving the aggressive analyst his due, because he's not wrong about everything.

On fundamentals: Microsoft's 38% net margins, 34% ROE, 18% revenue growth, and 23% EPS growth are genuinely exceptional. These numbers are real, and they reflect a company with powerful competitive advantages. The fortress balance sheet, the $46.68B quarterly operating cash flow—these are facts that matter.

On analyst consensus: The 60+ analyst buy ratings are not random noise. These analysts have access to management guidance, channel checks, and industry data that we don't have. The consensus for 25-27% Azure growth is a meaningful signal that someone with visibility into Microsoft's business believes growth is accelerating.

On the capex justification narrative: The aggressive analyst's argument that capex acceleration reflects "capital confidence" rather than "capital distress" is logically sound if Azure demand is genuinely strong. A company with visibility into strong demand should invest aggressively to capture market share.

On timing: The aggressive analyst is correct that earnings are days away, and by the time the market has consensus confirmation, a significant portion of any positive move may have already occurred. This is a legitimate point about opportunity cost.

These points are all valid. And they do support a position larger than 20%.

Where the Conservative Analyst Is Actually Correct¶

Now let me give the conservative analyst his due as well.

On the capex-to-deferred revenue divergence: The conservative analyst is absolutely right that capex grew 84% while deferred revenue grew 15%, and that divergence is not easily explained away by accounting technicalities. When two metrics that should theoretically be aligned diverge that sharply, it deserves serious consideration. The aggressive analyst's "normalization" arguments may be technically sound, but they introduce so many layers of adjustment that the original data signal gets lost.

On free cash flow deterioration: The fact that FCF fell from $25.66B to $15.80B while capex doubled is a legitimate warning signal. The conservative analyst is right that when a company is drawing down cash reserves to fund both capex and shareholder returns, you're in a situation where cash generation capacity is genuinely constrained. That's not "capital confidence"—that's the company deploying capital at the edge of its generation capacity.

On competitive risk: The conservative analyst's point that capex arms races historically lead to margin compression, not margin expansion, is historically grounded. Cisco didn't go bankrupt, but it never maintained the pricing power it had pre-2000. That's a real precedent.

On technical setup: The StochRSI has genuinely been in extreme overbought territory for 10 trading days. Historically, that does precede consolidation or pullback. The conservative analyst is right that positioning ahead of that kind of technical setup is riskier than positioning after it resolves.

These points are all valid. And they do suggest that oversizing ahead of an earnings catalyst is imprudent.

Where Both Analysts Are Missing the Mark¶

Now here's what's actually frustrating about this debate: both analysts are overstating their certainty about unknowable outcomes.

The aggressive analyst is overstating confidence in the capex ROI thesis. He keeps saying "the data supports" 60-65% positioning, but the data doesn't actually prove that the capex will generate acceptable returns. The data shows that capex is being deployed and that analyst consensus expects strong growth. But consensus expectations frequently miss. The aggressive analyst hasn't accounted for the probability that this capex is a mistake—not a small miscalculation, but a genuine overinvestment that will compress margins and reduce returns for 3-4 years.

The conservative analyst is overstating confidence in the capex failure thesis. He keeps saying the "aggregate risk picture" demands 20% positioning, but he's treating concerning signals as proof of failure when they're actually just warning flags. Capex acceleration could indicate overinvestment, but it could also indicate appropriate competitive response to an AI infrastructure arms race. The conservative analyst hasn't adequately accounted for the probability that Microsoft's aggressive capex deployment is exactly the right call and will position the company to dominate AI workloads for the next 5 years.

Both are conflating "concerning data points" with "certain outcomes."

What the Actual Probability Distribution Probably Looks Like¶

Let me reweight the scenarios based on what I think is the most intellectually honest assessment of the evidence:

Scenario A (Azure growth 28%+ YoY, capex ROI validates, stock moves to $450-470): I'd estimate this at 30-35% probability, not 25-30% (aggressive estimate) or 45-55% (what either analyst is implicitly assuming). Why? Because analyst consensus is for 25-27%, and beating consensus by 1-3% is possible but not the base case. And even if Azure beats, the market may not extrapolate to full capex vindication until the following quarter.

Scenario B (Azure growth 24-26% YoY, in-line with expectations, capex ROI uncertain, stock consolidates at $390-410): I'd estimate this at 50-55% probability. This is the most likely outcome. Management guides in-line. Capex story remains unresolved. Market waits for next quarter for clarity. Stock moves sideways.

Scenario C (Azure growth 22% or lower YoY, disappointment relative to consensus, stock corrects to $370-385): I'd estimate this at 10-15% probability. This is a genuine disappointment scenario requiring macro headwinds or competitive loss to manifest. Possible but not probable.

Notice: my base case (Scenario B) is more probable than the aggressive analyst's estimate, and far more probable than the conservative analyst's implied estimate. But Scenario B doesn't offer dramatic upside. It offers consolidation.

Why This Probability Distribution Changes the Position Sizing Calculus¶

If Scenario B is 50-55% probable (not 65% as the aggressive analyst claims, not 40% as the conservative analyst implies), then the position sizing question becomes: what's the appropriate size for a thesis where the base case is modest upside in a consolidating market?

Here's the answer: 40-50% is appropriate, not 20% and not 60-65%.

Here's why:

If you take a 20% position and Scenario B plays out, you capture 1.6-2.4% portfolio upside in a base case that's 50% probable. That's only 0.8-1.2% expected value from the base case alone. That's undersized for a thesis that works.

If you take a 60% position and Scenario B plays out, you capture 4.8-7.2% portfolio upside in a base case that's 50% probable. That's 2.4-3.6% expected value from the base case. That's appropriate for a thesis that works. But you're also taking 2.76% downside risk on a 10-15% tail case, which may be too much risk for that return profile.

A 45% position splits the difference:

  • If Scenario B plays out (50% prob): You capture 3.6-5.4% portfolio upside = 1.8-2.7% expected value
  • If Scenario A plays out (32% prob): You capture 6.8-10.2% portfolio upside = 2.2-3.3% expected value
  • If Scenario C plays out (15% prob): You take 0.99% portfolio loss (45% position × 2.2% downside)

The expected return is approximately +4.0-6.0%, with asymmetric downside protection. That's a sensible risk-reward profile for the actual probability distribution.

Based on my assessment of the evidence and probabilities, here's what the trader should actually do:

This reflects: - Genuine conviction in the thesis (fundamentals are strong, analyst consensus is real) - Appropriate caution about unknowns (capex ROI unproven, technical setup overbought, base case is consolidation not explosion) - Proper sizing for a 50-55% probable base case that still works

  • If Azure beats expectations (comes in at 27%+ with positive guidance), add 20-25% to get to 65-70% total. You've now captured the upside from Scenario A.
  • If Azure is in-line (comes in at 25-26% with guided for continued strength), hold 45% and reassess next quarter. The base case is playing out.
  • If Azure disappoints (comes in at 23% or lower), do NOT add. Use the dip at $375-380 to add 10% only if guidance remains intact. Your hard stop is $385 for the original 45%.

Hold 20% powder for true alternatives or defensive deployment.

Stop Loss: $380 (1.8% downside from $393). This is between the 50 SMA ($401.35) and the 20-day Bollinger midline ($381.25). It's a real technical support level where you'd exit if the thesis breaks.

I'm not overconfident in either direction.

The Critical Distinction: Evidence vs. Certainty¶

Here's what I want to leave both analysts with:

The aggressive analyst has presented evidence that Azure is strong and capex is justified. But evidence is not certainty. The capex-to-deferred revenue divergence is also evidence, evidence of a different story. Both data points are real. Both deserve weighting.

The conservative analyst has presented evidence that the capex divergence is a warning signal. But a warning signal is not proof of failure. Many aggressive capex deployments work out; some don't. We don't know which category Microsoft falls into yet.

The honest answer is: we don't know if this capex deployment will work out. The base case suggests it will be fine (consolidation, eventually vindication). But there's meaningful tail risk that it won't be (competitive commoditization, margin compression).

Given that uncertainty, 45% with 35% confirmation powder is the most defensible position. It's not conservative enough to leave money on the table in favorable scenarios. It's not aggressive enough to expose the firm to catastrophic downside in tail scenarios. It's appropriately sized for genuine ambiguity.

Both the aggressive and conservative analysts have made intellectually sharp arguments. But I notice something: neither has truly acknowledged how much of this debate comes down to probability weighting of unknowable outcomes, not data interpretation.

The aggressive analyst is confident Scenario A and B are highly probable and Scenario C is unlikely.

The conservative analyst is confident Scenario B and C are more probable and Scenario A is unlikely.

Scenario C requires simultaneous execution failure, competitive loss, AND macro deterioration. The joint probability of all three is lower than the sum of individual risk components.

This is not a decision between "aggressive growth" and "capital protection." It's a decision about how to size a thesis when the conviction is real but incomplete.

Microsoft's fundamentals are strong. Azure momentum is visible. The capex is being deployed. But the ROI story is unproven. Both of these statements are true simultaneously.

A 50% position with post-earnings scaling gives you: - Meaningful exposure to capture upside if the thesis works (27.5% × 11% × 50% = +1.51% expected value in Scenario A) - Capital protection if the thesis breaks (+0.55% maximum loss in Scenario C) - Optionality to scale on confirmation (you have 30% powder to add if earnings validate) - Flexibility to cut quickly if earnings disappoint (you can exit 50% at market if Azure growth disappoints)

This is the approach that serves both growth objectives and capital preservation. It's not the most aggressive positioning (that's 70%), but it's far from the most conservative (that's 25%). And it's calibrated to what the evidence actually supports.

My Final Word¶

Execute 50% at $393 with the contingencies I've outlined.

This position sizes appropriately for the evidence we have, respects the genuine uncertainties we face, and maintains the flexibility to evolve as the thesis is validated or breaks down over the coming weeks.

That's the recommendation I'm standing behind. It's defensible, it's disciplined, and it balances conviction with prudence without sacrificing either.

That distribution justifies meaningful but not maximum sizing — the aggressive analyst's 70% call priced tail risk too low (assuming orderly 4.6% stops when a thesis break realistically costs ~8-10%), while the conservative 20-25% call undersizes an 80%-favorable thesis.
